Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Taking care of my mom on top of work has thrown my whole life off, and I feel like I’m not handling any of it the way I usually would. Ever since her stroke, trying to balance everything has just been too much.\n\nDoctor: How long after your mom’s stroke did these feelings start, and have they lasted less than a month or longer than a month?\n\nPatient: They started pretty quickly, within a few weeks of her stroke once I had to start juggling her hospital visits with my shifts at the shop. And it's definitely been longer than a month—this has been going on for about four months now.\n\nDoctor: Since these stress-related feelings started, have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself, or have you been unable to keep up with work or daily responsibilities because of them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any thoughts of hurting myself, but I'm really struggling to keep up with my responsibilities. I actually had a scary near-miss safety incident at the shop because I was so distracted, and my supervisor has already warned me about my attitude with my coworkers. I've also had to completely drop out of church and my truck club because I'm just running on fumes.\n\nDoctor: Since your mom’s stroke, have you been feeling mostly sad, hopeless, or very anxious and worried most days?\n\nPatient: I've been feeling completely overwhelmed and strung out trying to balance it all. I end up crying in the restroom at work between oil changes, and then I lie awake at night beating myself up because I feel like I'm dropping every single ball. It's just this constant, heavy stress and sadness that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these symptoms started within 3 months of your mom’s stroke and are mainly tied to that stressor, rather than happening without it?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. They started within just a few weeks of her stroke, right when I had to start juggling her care with my shifts at the shop. None of this was happening before, and it is completely tied to trying to handle this overwhelming situation.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings and problems gone away at all when the stress is lighter, or do they stay present even during breaks from caregiving and work?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had any breaks or lighter periods of stress over the last four months because the caregiving and work demands are completely constant. However, I keep telling myself that once my mom's health stabilizes and she doesn't need this level of care anymore, I'll be able to get back to my normal routine. I expect I'll stop feeling so strung out once things finally settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel these symptoms would likely improve if your mom’s health and caregiving demands got better, or do you think they would keep going even after the stress is over?\n\nPatient: I absolutely think they would get better. I keep telling myself that if and when my mom's health stabilizes and she doesn't need this level of care anymore, I'll be able to get back to my normal routine. I fully expect I'll stop feeling so strung out all the time once that weight is lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work, at home, or in your relationships in a way that feels out of proportion to what you would normally expect from this stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has completely derailed how I function day-to-day. I'm snapping at coworkers, crying in the shop restroom, and even had a dangerous near-miss at work because I was so distracted. I’ve also completely dropped out of my church and truck club, which is totally unlike me because those activities used to keep me grounded.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of wanting to die, hurting yourself, or feeling that life is not worth living since this started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any thoughts of hurting myself or wanting to die at all. I am just feeling extremely overwhelmed and completely exhausted by this whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any other major stressors around the same time, or do your symptoms seem to be mainly a reaction to your mom’s stroke and the caregiving/work strain?\n\nPatient: No, there aren't any other stressors in my life right now. Everything started when my mom had her stroke four months ago, and how I'm feeling is entirely a reaction to the sudden, overwhelming strain of caregiving on top of my demanding full-time job at the shop.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling overwhelmed, have you also been feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or anxious most days since this started?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels like my whole life has been yanked inside out and I'm constantly anxious. I find myself crying in the restroom at work between oil changes, and then I lie awake at night beating myself up over everything. It is just a very heavy, sad feeling that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period during this time when the symptoms were much worse right after the stressor started and then began to ease up, or have they stayed at about the same level the whole time?"

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Taking care of my mom on top of work has thrown my whole life off, and I feel like I’m not handling any of it the way I usually would. And it's definitely been longer than a month—this has been going on for about four months now.\n\nDoctor: Since these stress-related feelings started, have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself, or have you been unable to keep up with work or daily responsibilities because of them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any thoughts of hurting myself, but I'm really struggling to keep up with my responsibilities. I actually had a scary near-miss safety incident at the shop because I was so distracted, and my supervisor has already warned me about my attitude with my coworkers. I've also had to completely drop out of church and my truck club because I'm just running on fumes.\n\nDoctor: Since your mom’s stroke, have you been feeling mostly sad, hopeless, or very anxious and worried most days?\n\nPatient: I've been feeling completely overwhelmed and strung out trying to balance it all. I end up crying in the restroom at work between oil changes, and then I lie awake at night beating myself up because I feel like I'm dropping every single ball. It's just this constant, heavy stress and sadness that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these symptoms started within 3 months of your mom’s stroke and are mainly tied to that stressor, rather than happening without it?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. They started within just a few weeks of her stroke, right when I had to start juggling her care with my shifts at the shop. None of this was happening before, and it is completely tied to trying to handle this overwhelming situation.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings and problems gone away at all when the stress is lighter, or do they stay present even during breaks from caregiving and work?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had any breaks or lighter periods of stress over the last four months because the caregiving and work demands are completely constant. However, I keep telling myself that once my mom's health stabilizes and she doesn't need this level of care anymore, I'll be able to get back to my normal routine. I expect I'll stop feeling so strung out once things finally settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel these symptoms would likely improve if your mom’s health and caregiving demands got better, or do you think they would keep going even after the stress is over?\n\nPatient: I absolutely think they would get better. I keep telling myself that if and when my mom's health stabilizes and she doesn't need this level of care anymore, I'll be able to get back to my normal routine. I fully expect I'll stop feeling so strung out all the time once that weight is lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work, at home, or in your relationships in a way that feels out of proportion to what you would normally expect from this stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has completely derailed how I function day-to-day. I'm snapping at coworkers, crying in the shop restroom, and even had a dangerous near-miss at work because I was so distracted. I’ve also completely dropped out of my church and truck club, which is totally unlike me because those activities used to keep me grounded.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of wanting to die, hurting yourself, or feeling that life is not worth living since this started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any thoughts of hurting myself or wanting to die at all. I am just feeling extremely overwhelmed and completely exhausted by this whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any other major stressors around the same time, or do your symptoms seem to be mainly a reaction to your mom’s stroke and the caregiving/work strain?\n\nPatient: No, there aren't any other stressors in my life right now. Everything started when my mom had her stroke four months ago, and how I'm feeling is entirely a reaction to the sudden, overwhelming strain of caregiving on top of my demanding full-time job at the shop.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling overwhelmed, have you also been feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or anxious most days since this started?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels like my whole life has been yanked inside out and I'm constantly anxious. I find myself crying in the restroom at work between oil changes, and then I lie awake at night beating myself up over everything. It is just a very heavy, sad feeling that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period during this time when the symptoms were much worse right after the stressor started and then began to ease up, or have they stayed at about the same level the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't eased up at all. They have stayed consistently intense, if not worse, over the last four months because the caregiving demands are constant and never stop. The exhaustion has just kept building up, and I feel like I am constantly running on fumes.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you also noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that feel more than just being tired from being busy?"

After a near-miss safety incident at work and feedback from her pastor and a close friend that she seemed unlike herself and was \"running on fumes,\" she agreed to see a mental health professional her primary care doctor recommended to help her cope with the ongoing situation.\n- Chief Complaint: “Taking care of my mom on top of work has thrown my whole life off, and I feel like I’m not handling any of it the way I usually would.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S081 (Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response): Ever since my mom’s stroke, it feels like my whole life’s been yanked inside out—I snap at guys at the shop over little mistakes, cry in the bathroom between oil changes, bail on church and truck nights, and then lie awake beating myself up because I’m dropping balls at home and at work even though I’m running as hard as I can.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- symptoms_within_3_months_of_stressor: Within a few weeks of my mom’s stroke, once I started juggling her hospital visits with my shifts at the shop, I noticed I was crying in the restroom at work, snapping at people over nothing, and skipping out on church and truck nights because I just couldn’t handle anything extra.\n- resolve_within_6_months_after_stressor_ends: I keep telling myself that if and when my mom stabilizes or doesn’t need this level of care anymore, I expect I’ll be able to get back to my normal routine and not feel this strung out all the time.\n- functional_impairment: I’ve already had a near-miss at work because I was distracted thinking about my mom’s meds, my supervisor has warned me about my attitude with coworkers, and I’ve pretty much dropped out of church activities and truck club meets that used to keep me grounded.\n- duration: These problems have been going on for about four months now, ever since my mom had her stroke and I stepped in as her main caregiver.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Taking care of my mom on top of work has thrown my whole life off, and I feel like I’m not handling any of it the way I usually would.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: Patient Background and Presenting Concern, S081 (Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response), symptoms_within_3_months_of_stressor, functional_impairment\nAnswer strategy: Acknowledge that you have stopped participating in the activities you normally love, such as your classic-truck club events, social rides, and church activities. Maintain a tired and overwhelmed tone."

After a near-miss safety incident at work and feedback from her pastor and a close friend that she seemed unlike herself and was \"running on fumes,\" she agreed to see a mental health professional her primary care doctor recommended to help her cope with the ongoing situation.\n- Chief Complaint: “Taking care of my mom on top of work has thrown my whole life off, and I feel like I’m not handling any of it the way I usually would.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S081 (Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response): Ever since my mom’s stroke, it feels like my whole life’s been yanked inside out—I snap at guys at the shop over little mistakes, cry in the bathroom between oil changes, bail on church and truck nights, and then lie awake beating myself up because I’m dropping balls at home and at work even though I’m running as hard as I can.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- symptoms_within_3_months_of_stressor: Within a few weeks of my mom’s stroke, once I started juggling her hospital visits with my shifts at the shop, I noticed I was crying in the restroom at work, snapping at people over nothing, and skipping out on church and truck nights because I just couldn’t handle anything extra.\n- resolve_within_6_months_after_stressor_ends: I keep telling myself that if and when my mom stabilizes or doesn’t need this level of care anymore, I expect I’ll be able to get back to my normal routine and not feel this strung out all the time.\n- functional_impairment: I’ve already had a near-miss at work because I was distracted thinking about my mom’s meds, my supervisor has warned me about my attitude with coworkers, and I’ve pretty much dropped out of church activities and truck club meets that used to keep me grounded.\n- duration: These problems have been going on for about four months now, ever since my mom had her stroke and I stepped in as her main caregiver.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Taking care of my mom on top of work has thrown my whole life off, and I feel like I’m not handling any of it the way I usually would.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(No match in profile or inference only)\nAnswer strategy: State that you have never gone through a major, overwhelming stressor like this in the past, so you don't have a previous experience to compare it to."

What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Taking care of my mom on top of work has thrown my whole life off, and I feel like I’m not handling any of it the way I usually would. Ever since her stroke, trying to balance everything has just been too much.\n\nDoctor: How long after your mom’s stroke did these feelings start, and have they lasted less than a month or longer than a month?\n\nPatient: They started pretty quickly, within a few weeks of her stroke once I had to start juggling her hospital visits with my shifts at the shop. And it's definitely been longer than a month—this has been going on for about four months now.\n\nDoctor: Since these stress-related feelings started, have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself, or have you been unable to keep up with work or daily responsibilities because of them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any thoughts of hurting myself, but I'm really struggling to keep up with my responsibilities. I actually had a scary near-miss safety incident at the shop because I was so distracted, and my supervisor has already warned me about my attitude with my coworkers. I've also had to completely drop out of church and my truck club because I'm just running on fumes.\n\nDoctor: Since your mom’s stroke, have you been feeling mostly sad, hopeless, or very anxious and worried most days?\n\nPatient: I've been feeling completely overwhelmed and strung out trying to balance it all. It's just this constant, heavy stress and sadness that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these symptoms started within 3 months of your mom’s stroke and are mainly tied to that stressor, rather than happening without it?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. They started within just a few weeks of her stroke, right when I had to start juggling her care with my shifts at the shop. None of this was happening before, and it is completely tied to trying to handle this overwhelming situation.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings and problems gone away at all when the stress is lighter, or do they stay present even during breaks from caregiving and work?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had any breaks or lighter periods of stress over the last four months because the caregiving and work demands are completely constant. However, I keep telling myself that once my mom's health stabilizes and she doesn't need this level of care anymore, I'll be able to get back to my normal routine. I expect I'll stop feeling so strung out once things finally settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel these symptoms would likely improve if your mom’s health and caregiving demands got better, or do you think they would keep going even after the stress is over?\n\nPatient: I absolutely think they would get better. I keep telling myself that if and when my mom's health stabilizes and she doesn't need this level of care anymore, I'll be able to get back to my normal routine. I fully expect I'll stop feeling so strung out all the time once that weight is lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work, at home, or in your relationships in a way that feels out of proportion to what you would normally expect from this stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has completely derailed how I function day-to-day. I'm snapping at coworkers, crying in the shop restroom, and even had a dangerous near-miss at work because I was so distracted. I’ve also completely dropped out of my church and truck club, which is totally unlike me because those activities used to keep me grounded.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of wanting to die, hurting yourself, or feeling that life is not worth living since this started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any thoughts of hurting myself or wanting to die at all. I am just feeling extremely overwhelmed and completely exhausted by this whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any other major stressors around the same time, or do your symptoms seem to be mainly a reaction to your mom’s stroke and the caregiving/work strain?\n\nPatient: No, there aren't any other stressors in my life right now. Everything started when my mom had her stroke four months ago, and how I'm feeling is entirely a reaction to the sudden, overwhelming strain of caregiving on top of my demanding full-time job at the shop.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling overwhelmed, have you also been feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or anxious most days since this started?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels like my whole life has been yanked inside out and I'm constantly anxious. It is just a very heavy, sad feeling that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period during this time when the symptoms were much worse right after the stressor started and then began to ease up, or have they stayed at about the same level the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't eased up at all. They have stayed consistently intense, if not worse, over the last four months because the caregiving demands are constant and never stop. The exhaustion has just kept building up, and I feel like I am constantly running on fumes.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you also noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that feel more than just being tired from being busy?\n\nPatient: My sleep and energy have been completely wrecked. I lie awake at night beating myself up over everything I'm trying to manage, and I constantly feel like I'm running on fumes. I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite, though, since I'm just so focused on the exhaustion.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you also lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy, or has it been more mainly feeling stressed, overwhelmed, and sad because of the situation?\n\nPatient: It's not that I've lost interest in my truck club or church, because I really do miss them and want to go. I'm just so completely exhausted and overwhelmed by work and caregiving that I don't have the energy or the time to handle anything extra right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in the past when a major stressor caused similar symptoms, and did they improve once that stressor was over?"

After a near-miss safety incident at work and feedback from her pastor and a close friend that she seemed unlike herself and was \"running on fumes,\" she agreed to see a mental health professional her primary care doctor recommended to help her cope with the ongoing situation.\n- Chief Complaint: “Taking care of my mom on top of work has thrown my whole life off, and I feel like I’m not handling any of it the way I usually would.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S081 (Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response): Ever since my mom’s stroke, it feels like my whole life’s been yanked inside out—I snap at guys at the shop over little mistakes, cry in the bathroom between oil changes, bail on church and truck nights, and then lie awake beating myself up because I’m dropping balls at home and at work even though I’m running as hard as I can.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- symptoms_within_3_months_of_stressor: Within a few weeks of my mom’s stroke, once I started juggling her hospital visits with my shifts at the shop, I noticed I was crying in the restroom at work, snapping at people over nothing, and skipping out on church and truck nights because I just couldn’t handle anything extra.\n- resolve_within_6_months_after_stressor_ends: I keep telling myself that if and when my mom stabilizes or doesn’t need this level of care anymore, I expect I’ll be able to get back to my normal routine and not feel this strung out all the time.\n- functional_impairment: I’ve already had a near-miss at work because I was distracted thinking about my mom’s meds, my supervisor has warned me about my attitude with coworkers, and I’ve pretty much dropped out of church activities and truck club meets that used to keep me grounded.\n- duration: These problems have been going on for about four months now, ever since my mom had her stroke and I stepped in as her main caregiver.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Specifically mention having a near-miss safety incident at the shop because you were distracted thinking about your mom's medication, and that your supervisor has warned you about your attitude. I'm just so completely exhausted and overwhelmed by work and caregiving that I don't have the energy or the time to handle anything extra right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in the past when a major stressor caused similar symptoms, and did they improve once that stressor was over?\n\nPatient: No, I've never gone through a major, overwhelming stressor like this in the past. As weeks went by, she began turning down social rides, missing classic-truck events, and arguing with coworkers over minor issues as the strain of caregiving, mounting bills, and reduced personal time wore on her. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Taking care of my mom on top of work has thrown my whole life off, and I feel like I’m not handling any of it the way I usually would. Ever since her stroke, trying to balance everything has just been too much.\n\nDoctor: How long after your mom’s stroke did these feelings start, and have they lasted less than a month or longer than a month?\n\nPatient: They started pretty quickly, within a few weeks of her stroke once I had to start juggling her hospital visits with my shifts at the shop. And it's definitely been longer than a month—this has been going on for about four months now.\n\nDoctor: Since these stress-related feelings started, have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself, or have you been unable to keep up with work or daily responsibilities because of them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any thoughts of hurting myself, but I'm really struggling to keep up with my responsibilities. I actually had a scary near-miss safety incident at the shop because I was so distracted, and my supervisor has already warned me about my attitude with my coworkers. I've also had to completely drop out of church and my truck club because I'm just running on fumes.\n\nDoctor: Since your mom’s stroke, have you been feeling mostly sad, hopeless, or very anxious and worried most days?\n\nPatient: I've been feeling completely overwhelmed and strung out trying to balance it all. I end up crying in the restroom at work between oil changes, and then I lie awake at night beating myself up because I feel like I'm dropping every single ball. It's just this constant, heavy stress and sadness that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these symptoms started within 3 months of your mom’s stroke and are mainly tied to that stressor, rather than happening without it?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. They started within just a few weeks of her stroke, right when I had to start juggling her care with my shifts at the shop. None of this was happening before, and it is completely tied to trying to handle this overwhelming situation.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings and problems gone away at all when the stress is lighter, or do they stay present even during breaks from caregiving and work?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had any breaks or lighter periods of stress over the last four months because the caregiving and work demands are completely constant. However, I keep telling myself that once my mom's health stabilizes and she doesn't need this level of care anymore, I'll be able to get back to my normal routine. I expect I'll stop feeling so strung out once things finally settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel these symptoms would likely improve if your mom’s health and caregiving demands got better, or do you think they would keep going even after the stress is over?\n\nPatient: I absolutely think they would get better. I keep telling myself that if and when my mom's health stabilizes and she doesn't need this level of care anymore, I'll be able to get back to my normal routine. I fully expect I'll stop feeling so strung out all the time once that weight is lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work, at home, or in your relationships in a way that feels out of proportion to what you would normally expect from this stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has completely derailed how I function day-to-day. I'm snapping at coworkers, crying in the shop restroom, and even had a dangerous near-miss at work because I was so distracted. I’ve also completely dropped out of my church and truck club, which is totally unlike me because those activities used to keep me grounded.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of wanting to die, hurting yourself, or feeling that life is not worth living since this started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any thoughts of hurting myself or wanting to die at all. I am just feeling extremely overwhelmed and completely exhausted by this whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any other major stressors around the same time, or do your symptoms seem to be mainly a reaction to your mom’s stroke and the caregiving/work strain?\n\nPatient: No, there aren't any other stressors in my life right now. Everything started when my mom had her stroke four months ago, and how I'm feeling is entirely a reaction to the sudden, overwhelming strain of caregiving on top of my demanding full-time job at the shop.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling overwhelmed, have you also been feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or anxious most days since this started?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels like my whole life has been yanked inside out and I'm constantly anxious. I find myself crying in the restroom at work between oil changes, and then I lie awake at night beating myself up over everything. It is just a very heavy, sad feeling that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period during this time when the symptoms were much worse right after the stressor started and then began to ease up, or have they stayed at about the same level the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't eased up at all. They have stayed consistently intense, if not worse, over the last four months because the caregiving demands are constant and never stop. The exhaustion has just kept building up, and I feel like I am constantly running on fumes.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you also noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that feel more than just being tired from being busy?\n\nPatient: My sleep and energy have been completely wrecked. I lie awake at night beating myself up over everything I'm trying to manage, and I constantly feel like I'm running on fumes. I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite, though, since I'm just so focused on the exhaustion.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you also lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy, or has it been more mainly feeling stressed, overwhelmed, and sad because of the situation?\n\nPatient: It's not that I've lost interest in my truck club or church, because I really do miss them and want to go. I'm just so completely exhausted and overwhelmed by work and caregiving that I don't have the energy or the time to handle anything extra right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in the past when a major stressor caused similar symptoms, and did they improve once that stressor was over?\n\nPatient: No, I've never gone through a major, overwhelming stressor like this in the past. I don't really have a previous experience to compare it to, since my life was pretty stable and predictable before my mom's stroke.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to miss work, make major mistakes, or struggle to take care of basic responsibilities at home because of the stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I had a dangerous near-miss safety incident at the shop because I was distracted thinking about my mom's meds, and my supervisor warned me about my attitude with coworkers. I also feel like I'm constantly dropping balls at home and have completely stopped participating in my usual church and truck club activities because I'm just so overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any similar episodes of feeling overwhelmed, sad, or unable to cope before this situation with your mom's stroke, even during other stressful times in your life?"

I'm just so completely exhausted and overwhelmed by work and caregiving that I don't have the energy or the time to handle anything extra right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in the past when a major stressor caused similar symptoms, and did they improve once that stressor was over?\n\nPatient: No, I've never gone through a major, overwhelming stressor like this in the past. I've always been able to handle my life and routine normally, and I've never had issues coping with stress until my mom's stroke. This is completely new for me.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present continuously for less than 6 months, or have they lasted 6 months or longer?"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Taking care of my mom on top of work has thrown my whole life off, and I feel like I’m not handling any of it the way I usually would. Ever since her stroke, trying to balance everything has just been too much.\n\nDoctor: How long after your mom’s stroke did these feelings start, and have they lasted less than a month or longer than a month?\n\nPatient: They started pretty quickly, within a few weeks of her stroke once I had to start juggling her hospital visits with my shifts at the shop. And it's definitely been longer than a month—this has been going on for about four months now.\n\nDoctor: Since these stress-related feelings started, have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself, or have you been unable to keep up with work or daily responsibilities because of them?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any thoughts of hurting myself, but I'm really struggling to keep up with my responsibilities. I actually had a scary near-miss safety incident at the shop because I was so distracted, and my supervisor has already warned me about my attitude with my coworkers. I've also had to completely drop out of church and my truck club because I'm just running on fumes.\n\nDoctor: Since your mom’s stroke, have you been feeling mostly sad, hopeless, or very anxious and worried most days?\n\nPatient: I've been feeling completely overwhelmed and strung out trying to balance it all. I end up crying in the restroom at work between oil changes, and then I lie awake at night beating myself up because I feel like I'm dropping every single ball. It's just this constant, heavy stress and sadness that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these symptoms started within 3 months of your mom’s stroke and are mainly tied to that stressor, rather than happening without it?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. They started within just a few weeks of her stroke, right when I had to start juggling her care with my shifts at the shop. None of this was happening before, and it is completely tied to trying to handle this overwhelming situation.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings and problems gone away at all when the stress is lighter, or do they stay present even during breaks from caregiving and work?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had any breaks or lighter periods of stress over the last four months because the caregiving and work demands are completely constant. However, I keep telling myself that once my mom's health stabilizes and she doesn't need this level of care anymore, I'll be able to get back to my normal routine. I expect I'll stop feeling so strung out once things finally settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel these symptoms would likely improve if your mom’s health and caregiving demands got better, or do you think they would keep going even after the stress is over?\n\nPatient: I absolutely think they would get better. I keep telling myself that if and when my mom's health stabilizes and she doesn't need this level of care anymore, I'll be able to get back to my normal routine. I fully expect I'll stop feeling so strung out all the time once that weight is lifted.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms made it hard for you to function at work, at home, or in your relationships in a way that feels out of proportion to what you would normally expect from this stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has completely derailed how I function day-to-day. I'm snapping at coworkers, crying in the shop restroom, and even had a dangerous near-miss at work because I was so distracted. I’ve also completely dropped out of my church and truck club, which is totally unlike me because those activities used to keep me grounded.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of wanting to die, hurting yourself, or feeling that life is not worth living since this started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any thoughts of hurting myself or wanting to die at all. I am just feeling extremely overwhelmed and completely exhausted by this whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any other major stressors around the same time, or do your symptoms seem to be mainly a reaction to your mom’s stroke and the caregiving/work strain?\n\nPatient: No, there aren't any other stressors in my life right now. Everything started when my mom had her stroke four months ago, and how I'm feeling is entirely a reaction to the sudden, overwhelming strain of caregiving on top of my demanding full-time job at the shop.\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling overwhelmed, have you also been feeling unusually sad, hopeless, or anxious most days since this started?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels like my whole life has been yanked inside out and I'm constantly anxious. I find myself crying in the restroom at work between oil changes, and then I lie awake at night beating myself up over everything. It is just a very heavy, sad feeling that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period during this time when the symptoms were much worse right after the stressor started and then began to ease up, or have they stayed at about the same level the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't eased up at all. They have stayed consistently intense, if not worse, over the last four months because the caregiving demands are constant and never stop. The exhaustion has just kept building up, and I feel like I am constantly running on fumes.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you also noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that feel more than just being tired from being busy?\n\nPatient: My sleep and energy have been completely wrecked. I lie awake at night beating myself up over everything I'm trying to manage, and I constantly feel like I'm running on fumes. I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite, though, since I'm just so focused on the exhaustion.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you also lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy, or has it been more mainly feeling stressed, overwhelmed, and sad because of the situation?\n\nPatient: It's not that I've lost interest in my truck club or church, because I really do miss them and want to go. I'm just so completely exhausted and overwhelmed by work and caregiving that I don't have the energy or the time to handle anything extra right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in the past when a major stressor caused similar symptoms, and did they improve once that stressor was over?\n\nPatient: No, I've never gone through a major, overwhelming stressor like this in the past. I don't really have a previous experience to compare it to, since my life was pretty stable and predictable before my mom's stroke.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to miss work, make major mistakes, or struggle to take care of basic responsibilities at home because of the stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I had a dangerous near-miss safety incident at the shop because I was distracted thinking about my mom's meds, and my supervisor warned me about my attitude with coworkers. I also feel like I'm constantly dropping balls at home and have completely stopped participating in my usual church and truck club activities because I'm just so overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any similar episodes of feeling overwhelmed, sad, or unable to cope before this situation with your mom's stroke, even during other stressful times in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I have never felt this way before. I've always been able to handle my life and routine normally, and I've never had issues coping with stress until my mom's stroke. This is completely new for me.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present continuously for less than 6 months, or have they lasted 6 months or longer?\n\nPatient: They've been going on for less than six months. It’s been about four months now, starting right after my mom had her stroke and I suddenly had to become her main caregiver.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms started to improve at all as you’ve gotten more used to the caregiving situation, or are they still ongoing at the same level?"

It is just a very heavy, sad feeling that I can't seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period during this time when the symptoms were much worse right after the stressor started and then began to ease up, or have they stayed at about the same level the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't eased up at all. They have stayed consistently intense, if not worse, over the last four months because the caregiving demands are constant and never stop. The exhaustion has just kept building up, and I feel like I am constantly running on fumes.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you also noticed changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that feel more than just being tired from being busy?\n\nPatient: My sleep and energy have been completely wrecked. I lie awake at night beating myself up over everything I'm trying to manage, and I constantly feel like I'm running on fumes. I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite, though, since I'm just so focused on the exhaustion.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you also lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy, or has it been more mainly feeling stressed, overwhelmed, and sad because of the situation?\n\nPatient: It's not that I've lost interest in my truck club or church, because I really do miss them and want to go. I'm just so completely exhausted and overwhelmed by work and caregiving that I don't have the energy or the time to handle anything extra right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in the past when a major stressor caused similar symptoms, and did they improve once that stressor was over?\n\nPatient: No, I've never gone through a major, overwhelming stressor like this in the past. I don't really have a previous experience to compare it to, since my life was pretty stable and predictable before my mom's stroke.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to miss work, make major mistakes, or struggle to take care of basic responsibilities at home because of the stress?\n\nPatient: Yes, I had a dangerous near-miss safety incident at the shop because I was distracted thinking about my mom's meds, and my supervisor warned me about my attitude with coworkers. I also feel like I'm constantly dropping balls at home and have completely stopped participating in my usual church and truck club activities because I'm just so overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any similar episodes of feeling overwhelmed, sad, or unable to cope before this situation with your mom's stroke, even during other stressful times in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I have never felt this way before. I've always been able to handle my life and routine normally, and I've never had issues coping with stress until my mom's stroke. This is completely new for me.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present continuously for less than 6 months, or have they lasted 6 months or longer?\n\nPatient: They've been going on for less than six months. It’s been about four months now, starting right after my mom had her stroke and I suddenly had to become her main caregiver.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms started to improve at all as you’ve gotten more used to the caregiving situation, or are they still ongoing at the same level?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't improved at all and are still ongoing at the same intense level. I feel just as overwhelmed as I did in the beginning because the caregiving demands haven't let up. I really think things will only start to get better once my mom's condition stabilizes or she doesn't need this level of constant care."

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol). Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.
